<note>This page contains no primary text or drawings. The faint, illegible marks visible in the original manuscript are the result of "ghosting"—a process where ink from the reverse side or an adjacent page has bled through or transferred onto this surface over time. In archival study, these mirror-images can sometimes help reconstruct lost text, but here they remain decorative traces of the document's age.</note>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the theoretical discussions of optics and perception mentioned on the previous page to practical, mechanical applications. The content on folio 53r focuses on acoustics and foundry machinery, specifically the mechanics of bells and lifting equipment.</meta>

# Folio 53r

-> brass bell <-
<note>original: "campana d'ottone"</note>

<note>The drawings on this folio explore the mechanics of bell-ringing and perhaps the casting or lifting of bells.</note>

### Description of Studies

**Upper Illustration:**
This is a detailed study of a bell suspended from a horizontal timber beam. A complex mechanical linkage is attached to the left side, featuring a series of articulated arms. One arm ends in a hammer or striker that rests against the "waist" or "sound bow" <note>the thickest part of a bell's rim where the most sound is produced</note> of the bell. This appears to be a design for an automated bell-ringing device or a mechanism to strike the bell without swinging it, likely for use in a clock tower.

**Middle-Left Diagram:**
A small circular sketch containing a four-armed or cruciform figure. This may represent a schematic view of the bell's interior suspension for the clapper or a plan view of the bell's crown.

**Middle-Right Sketch:**
A study of the bell's "crown" or "canons" <note>the loops at the top of a bell used for hanging it</note>, showing the decorative and functional curves of the bronze cast.

**Bottom Illustration:**
A large red chalk sketch of a vertical screw or spindle mechanism. It consists of a central threaded pole resting on a flat base, with two horizontal arms or levers near the top. This represents a screw jack <note>original: "vite"</note> used for lifting heavy weights, such as bells, or a component of a mold-making apparatus used in the bell foundry.

# Mechanical Study of a Structural Frame

->[Diagram of a trapezoidal hoisting frame with internal pulleys and supports]<-

**Proportional Notations:**

### Technical Analysis
The drawing demonstrates a sophisticated understanding of <term>mechanics</term> <note>the branch of physics dealing with the motion of objects and the forces acting upon them</note>. The architectural study reflects the contemporary interest in <term>symmetry</term> <note>the quality of being made up of exactly similar parts facing each other or around an axis</note>, suggesting a design for a domestic residence or a rural estate that harmonizes with its surroundings.

# Studies of Gear and Drum Mechanisms

<note>This page features a detailed mechanical study, likely for a clock mechanism or a lifting device, drawn in pen and ink with red chalk underdrawing. While there is no handwritten text by the artist, the drawings provide a clear visual narrative of engineering intent.</note>

->[Study of a gear and drum mechanism]<-

<note>This primary technical study shows a <term>toothed wheel</term> <note>original: ruota dentata</note> integrated with a vertical <term>drum</term> <note>original: tamburo</note>. <column-break/>

->[Sketch of a suspended weight]<-

<note>Positioned below the main assembly, this diagram shows a cylindrical weight suspended by a cord. ### Technical Observations

The combination of media on this page—pen and ink over red chalk—suggests a two-stage process: an initial freehand exploration of the volume and placement, followed by a precise "inking in" of the functional mechanical edges. 

The central mechanism appears to be a study in mechanical advantage. By connecting a large-diameter drum to a smaller-toothed gear, the artist explores how to regulate the descent of a weight to provide a steady, controlled release of energy. This principle was essential for both the development of accurate clocks and the creation of cranes capable of lifting heavy architectural elements.

<note>Top grouping of harmonic ratios</note>

6
3
1

6
4
2
1

<note>These sequences represent basic arithmetic and geometric progressions. Leonardo used these numbers to visualize the relationships between different lengths of strings or pipes. For example, the relationship of 2:1 represents an octave, while 4:2 and 6:3 also simplify to this same fundamental harmonic doubling.</note>

***

->Music for the <term>piva</term><-
<note>original: "musicha di piva." A *piva* was a popular Renaissance wind instrument, typically a type of bagpipe or a small rustic pipe. Leonardo was a master of the "lira da braccio" and was deeply interested in the physical construction and tuning of instruments.</note>

***

<note>Diagram of musical intervals (the perfect fifth, 3:2 ratio)</note>

12 ——— 8
6 ——— 4
3 ——— 2
9 ——— 6

<note>This table illustrates the <term>sesquialtera</term> <note>original: "sesquialtera"</note> proportion, which is the ratio of 3:2. In music theory, this specific mathematical relationship produces the interval of a "perfect fifth" (the distance, for example, between the notes C and G). By listing different numerical pairs—12:8, 6:4, and 9:6—Leonardo demonstrates that regardless of the specific size or weight of the vibrating object, as long as this 3:2 proportion is maintained, the resulting harmony remains constant.</note>

***

**Technical Vocabulary:**
*   **Music for the piva:** Refers to compositions or tuning scales for the Renaissance bagpipe.
*   **Proportion:** The mathematical relationship between two quantities.
*   **Sesquialtera:** A ratio of 1.5 to 1 (or 3:2), essential for defining harmonic fifths.
*   **Musical intervals:** The difference in pitch between two sounds.

<summary>A mathematical study of harmonic proportions and musical intervals, likely intended for the tuning or design of a Renaissance wind instrument known as a piva. Leonardo demonstrates how the 3:2 ratio (sesquialtera) forms the basis of the perfect fifth through various numerical pairings.</summary>
<keywords>music theory, piva, bagpipe, harmonic ratios, sesquialtera, perfect fifth, Leonardo da Vinci, acoustics, mathematics</keywords>

<note>original: "proportione discreta". Unlike a continuous proportion where the middle term is shared, a discrete proportion involves four distinct terms where the first is to the second as the third is to the fourth.</note>

-> 1 4 10 20 <-

<note>The arcs connect the pairs 1–4 and 10–20. # perspective

<note>The following text and diagram are executed in red chalk (sanguine). The text is written in Leonardo's characteristic mirror script (right-to-left).</note>

<note>Diagram showing linear perspective: an eye (represented by a circle on the right) observes four vertical objects of equal height that appear smaller as they recede into the distance. Lines of sight converge from the objects to the eye. The objects are labeled with letters: f, p, c, o (from left to right).</note>

All things [moving] through the air
that recede from the eye
continue to diminish.
smoke <note>original: "fumo". Leonardo often observed how smoke or haze affected the "perspective of disappearance," where objects not only get smaller but also lose definition.</note>

---

The mirror shows
all things that are before it,
and how one [object] behind another
is hidden within the mirror. <note>Leonardo is fascinated by how a flat mirror can accurately represent <term>occlusion</term>—the way foreground objects block our view of background objects—maintaining the illusion of three-dimensional depth.</note>

<note>Technical vocabulary: perspective, air, eye, diminishing, smoke, mirror, in front of, hide/conceal.</note>

<summary>Leonardo illustrates the geometric laws of linear perspective, showing how objects of equal height appear smaller as they recede, and notes how mirrors faithfully replicate spatial depth and the overlapping of objects.</summary>

# Folio 25

### Study of Torsion and Geometric Solids

<note>This page features a detailed study in red chalk of a rectangular prism being twisted. Leonardo is investigating how the straight edges of a solid transform into curves when subjected to rotational force. This type of study is foundational for both his architectural theories and his understanding of human anatomy.</note>

<margin>
towards the other
</margin>

let it become a **rhombus**
with **curved sides**

<note>original: "rōbo curuo di coste" — The word "rōbo" is a shorthand for "rombo" (rhombus). Leonardo is describing how the face of a rectangular prism deforms into a curved-edged rhombus as the entire structure is twisted.</note>

<column-break/>

### Technical Vocabulary

*   **Rhombus** <term>original: "rombo" — a four-sided shape where all sides are equal in length. In this study, Leonardo observes how a square or rectangular face takes on this shape during torsion.</term>
*   **Sides** <term>original: "coste" — literally "ribs," a term Leonardo frequently uses to describe the structural edges or the "skeleton" of a geometric form.</term>
*   **Curved** <term>original: "curvo" — indicating the transition from linear geometry to curvilinear forms, a recurring theme in Leonardo’s studies of nature and mechanics.</term>

<note>The drawing shows internal diagonal lines within the prism, which illustrate the internal stresses and the rotation of the shape's volume around a central axis.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the geometric transformation of a rectangular prism as it undergoes torsion, showing how straight edges deform into curves. It highlights Leonardo's method of using red chalk to visualize the intersection of mathematical theory and physical mechanics.</summary>

->**Botanical and Geometric Observations**<-

This page serves as a workspace for Leonardo’s observations of organic growth and mathematical structure. The primary focus is a delicate study in <term>sanguine</term> <note>A reddish-brown chalk made from earth pigments, favored by Renaissance artists for its ability to create soft gradations.</note> of a stalk of grain. This juxtaposition of a rigid mathematical frame with a flowing, organic line is a hallmark of Leonardo’s attempt to find the "universal laws" that govern both art and nature.

# Page 42

<note>This page features a series of rough sketches executed in red chalk, also known as <term>sanguine</term> (a traditional drawing material made of earth pigments). These are preparatory anatomical and figure studies. There is no written text on this folio, with the exception of the archival page number "42" in the upper right corner.</note>

***

### Anatomical and Figure Studies

->[Illustration: A fluid red chalk sketch. The light, energetic lines suggest a human torso or a reclining figure, capturing the essence of posture and organic form with great economy of stroke. This is a characteristic example of Leonardo's use of red chalk for rapid observation, likely for a larger composition such as the *Battle of Anghiari*.]<-

<column-break/>

->[Illustration: An anatomical sketch focusing on the musculature of a joint area, possibly the hip or shoulder. Leonardo employs diagonal <term>hatching</term> (parallel lines used to create shade) to give the form volume and weight, revealing his analytical approach to representing the mechanics of the human body.]<-

<column-break/>

->[Illustration: A quick sketch in red chalk capturing the form of a bent joint, likely a knee. These small studies served as vital reference points for Leonardo's more complex anatomical diagrams and figure paintings.]<-

<summary>This page contains a collection of red chalk (sanguine) sketches by Leonardo da Vinci, focusing on the human torso and the musculature of joints. These drawings highlight his method of using rapid figure studies and detailed anatomical hatching to understand physical mechanics.</summary>

# Page 25

<note>This page consists of several mechanical and geometric diagrams rendered in red chalk and brown ink. These sketches focus on the principles of balance and the law of the lever—a fundamental concept in physics where a smaller weight can balance a larger one depending on its placement.</note>

***

### Studies on the Law of the Lever

->[Illustration: Three diagrams at the top of the page show horizontal beams with weights suspended from them. These represent various states of <term>equilibrium</term> (a state where opposing forces are balanced).]<-

<note>Leonardo’s text here is written in his famous "mirror script," written from right to left.</note>

->**the distance**<-
<note>original: "la distantia"</note>

<column-break/>

<note>In the lower section of the page, the sketches become more complex, incorporating pulleys and arcs that track the path of a falling weight. Leonardo uses numerical ratios to calculate how much force is required to move a specific load.</note>

<margin>
3
—
2
</margin>
<note>These figures represent the ratio of weight to distance. Leonardo is likely testing how a weight of 3 units at a distance of 2 units interacts with its counterpart.</note>

<margin>
3
—
6
</margin>
<note>This ratio suggests a relationship where the distance is doubled (from 3 to 6), which would significantly change the leverage of the weight.</note>

***

### Proportional Balance

->[Illustration: A series of arcs and intersecting lines showing the movement of a weighted arm. Below these, Leonardo has noted ratios that suggest he was calculating the <term>moment of force</term> (the measure of the tendency of a force to cause an object to rotate around a specific point).]<-

<note>Leonardo's use of both <term>sanguine</term> (red chalk) and ink suggests a layered process of thought, where initial geometric ideas were later refined with more precise mathematical notations.</note>

<summary>This page captures Leonardo da Vinci’s scientific investigation into the laws of statics and equilibrium. Through diagrams of weights and levers, he explores the mathematical relationship between distance and force, laying the groundwork for modern mechanical engineering.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, statics, law of the lever, equilibrium, physics, proportions, mirror-writing, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

# Page 41

<note>This page is dominated by two distinct drawings executed in <term>red chalk</term> (also known as sanguine), a favorite medium of Leonardo’s for its soft, expressive lines. The sketches represent a transition from observational drawing to pure mathematical inquiry.</note>

***

### Geometric and Spherical Study

<note>The upper portion of the page features a large, freehand circular sketch. This likely represents a study of a sphere or a celestial body. Leonardo often used such forms to investigate the way light falls on a curved surface or to explore the geometry of the eye and planetary movements. The heavier markings at the bottom suggest a horizon line or the shadowing of a solid object resting on a surface.</note>

***

### Numerical Proportions and the Pythagorean Principle

<note>The lower section contains a diagram enclosed in a light oval frame, focusing on numerical ratios. Leonardo was deeply interested in <term>proportions</term>—the mathematical relationship between different parts of a whole—which he applied to everything from architecture to the human face.</note>

->6<-

<unclear>d v n 2</unclear> 3
<note>These characters are difficult to decipher, but they appear to be shorthand notations or abbreviations accompanying his calculations.</note>

4 | 3 | 5
---|---|---
4 | 3 | 5

4 3 5

<note>The repetition of the numbers 3, 4, and 5 is highly significant. This refers to the <term>Pythagorean triple</term>, where a triangle with sides of these lengths always forms a perfect right angle ($3^2 + 4^2 = 5^2$). This principle was a cornerstone of classical geometry and Renaissance architectural design.</note>

->6<-

<margin>
5
</margin>

<note>The number 6, centered above and below the diagram, may represent a further step in a geometric progression or a specific measurement in Leonardo’s study of harmonic balance.</note>

These sketches reflect his search for the mathematical laws underpinning both physical shapes and spatial relationships.</summary>

<keywords>geometry, red chalk, proportions, Pythagorean triangle, circle, mathematical diagrams, sanguine</keywords>

<meta>This page follows Leonardo’s exploration of mathematical foundations and proportions. While the previous page (Page 41) examined the abstract laws of geometry and the Pythagorean principle of the 3-4-5 triangle, Page 24 applies these geometric interests to natural phenomena. # Page 24

<note>The top half of this page features a study of concentric circles executed in <term>red chalk</term> (also known as sanguine). Leonardo often used this medium for its ability to create soft, blending gradients. These circles are a hallmark of his investigations into <term>wave propagation</term>, illustrating how energy—whether in the form of sound, light, or water ripples—spreads outward from a central point of impact.</note>

***

<note>Below the geometric study are two lines of text written in Leonardo’s famous <term>mirror script</term>, running from right to left. The lines are poetic aphorisms, likely influenced by classical literature.</note>

***

<unclear>... of love ...</unclear> <note>original: "... d'amore ..."</note>

Love conquers all for those who possess it well <note>original: "tutto vince l'amor chi ha bene"</note>

Everything is conquered through love <note>original: "tutto si vince per l'amor"</note>

<note>These lines are Leonardo’s personal variations on the famous Latin aphorism "Amor vincit omnia" (Love conquers all), a sentiment originally from the Roman poet Virgil. In Leonardo's notebooks, such philosophical musings often sit alongside scientific diagrams, suggesting he saw no divide between "science" and "emotion."</note>

***

### Vocabulary Summary
*   **All/Everything** (<term>tutto</term>): Referring to the totality of experience or the universe.
*   **Conquers/Wins** (<term>vince</term>): From the verb "vincere," suggesting a triumph or mastery over obstacles.
*   **Love** (<term>amor</term>): A central theme in Leonardo's more literary or philosophical notes.
*   **Good/Well** (<term>bene</term>): Used here to imply either the possession of goodness or the proper application of love.

***

<summary>This page features a red chalk study of concentric circles, likely representing the physical laws of wave propagation, accompanied by philosophical aphorisms on the conquering power of love.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, red chalk, concentric circles, wave propagation, philosophy, Amor vincit omnia, mirror script, Italian Renaissance</keywords>

# Mechanical Studies on Rope and Interlocking Gears

<note>Leonardo uses the Latinate term "ductura" to describe the path or the "leading" of a rope through a mechanical system. This likely refers to the initial setup of a pulley or winding mechanism.</note>

<column-break/>

->[Diagram: An interlocking mechanism where two surfaces meet in a wavy, sinusoidal curve, housed within a rectangular frame.]<-

they are <note>original: "sono"</note>
</margin>

<note>The labels below refer to specific points or variables within the mechanical calculation.</note>

**v** <note>Likely "vite" (screw) or "velocità" (velocity)</note>
**c**
**p** <note>Likely "peso" (weight) or "potenza" (power/force)</note>

<note>The wavy profile of the interlocking parts is a sophisticated design intended to distribute pressure evenly or reduce the "stuttering" found in traditional square-toothed gears. Leonardo was obsessed with reducing friction, which he called the "mother of all waste."</note>

### Description of Drawings

The page features several faint sketches in red chalk:
*   **Top:** A schematic diagram of a mechanical device, possibly a lever, balance, or crane structure with a vertical support and a diagonal arm.
*   **Center:** A very light, gestural sketch of what appears to be a human torso or a limb in motion. The word "arm" written just above suggests this is an anatomical study of the arm's proportions or movement.
*   **Bottom:** A long, shallow curved line spanning the width of the page, with a small rectangular box or mechanism attached to its center. This may represent a bow, a structural beam, or a component of a flying machine.

<note>Leonardo often integrated his studies of "natural" mechanics (the human body) with "artificial" mechanics (machines), believing that the same laws of physics and proportion governed both.</note>

# Folio 49

<margin>water</margin>

Where the water strikes with its weight <note>original: "peso." Leonardo uses this term to describe the physical pressure or force exerted by the volume and movement of water.</note>
it requires stronger masonry construction;
and the embankment for the water, which is made of
large stone blocks <term>macigni: heavy, durable stone blocks, typically sandstone or greywacke, used for sturdy foundations</term>, and walling them in, and building
vaults above them, making a passage beneath them.

---

<note>
**The Architecture of Water Control**
The red chalk sketch on this page, though faint, illustrates Leonardo’s investigation into structural integrity for hydraulic projects. He recognizes that water exerts a constant force against any surface it hits. To counter this, he proposes a system using *macigni* (massive stones) and the construction of "volte" (vaults or arches). By creating a "passage beneath," he likely intends to allow water to flow through or under the structure, thereby reducing the direct impact and pressure on the main embankment or pier.
</note>

<summary>Leonardo describes the necessary reinforcement for embankments and water-retaining walls, advocating for the use of heavy stone blocks and vaulted passages to manage water pressure.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, masonry, water pressure, embankments, architecture, macigni, stone construction, engineering</keywords>

# Folio 57

<note>This page contains sketches of a mechanical frame and studies of pointed rods, drawn in red chalk (sanguine). The text is written in Leonardo's characteristic mirror script.</note>

<note>Above the vertical rod on the top left:</note>
thin and sharp <note>original: "sottili e aguti." Leonardo often investigated the geometry of points and needles, perhaps for textile machinery or precision tools.</note>

<note>Inside the mechanical frame on the left side:</note>
dry <note>original: "secco." In a structural context, this likely refers to the use of seasoned or "dry" wood to prevent warping or movement within the frame once it is assembled.</note>

<note>To the right of the mechanical frame:</note>
these
should not
be thin <note>original: "questi non deono essere sottili." This is a structural instruction, indicating that these specific vertical supports require more mass to maintain the integrity of the frame.</note>

---

<note>
**Structural Integrity and Precision**
In these sanguine (red chalk) sketches, Leonardo is thinking through the physical properties of a machine's skeleton. He contrasts different components: some must be "thin and sharp," suggesting they might be needles, pivots, or teeth that require precision. Others, specifically the main vertical supports of the frame, are marked with a warning that they "should not be thin," as they must bear the load of the mechanism without flexing. His note "dry" highlights his understanding of material science—using unseasoned wood in such a precise frame would lead to catastrophic misalignment as the wood dried and shrank over time.
</note>

<summary>Leonardo explores the material properties and structural requirements for a mechanical frame, specifying which parts must be sharp and thin versus those that require thickness and seasoned, "dry" material for stability.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, structural engineering, red chalk, sanguine, material properties, mirror script, carpentry, precision tools</keywords>

<note>The list is separated by a vertical line of decorative loops. The total sum (105) for the main burial expenses is calculated correctly based on the individual items. Additional expenses for wine and bread—likely for the funeral repast—bring the final total to 123 soldi.</note>

<note>Leonardo's use of "madre" (mother) here is one of the few places in his notebooks where he acknowledges his biological mother, Caterina, a peasant woman who had come to Milan to stay with her famous son before her death in 1494.</note>

<summary>A rare personal entry in Leonardo's notebooks detailing the itemized costs for the funeral and burial of his mother, Caterina, in Milan.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Caterina, funeral expenses, burial customs, Renaissance Milan, accounts, dowry, death announcement</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo's investigation into the physics of light and shadow, specifically focusing on how particulate matter like smoke and clouds interact with the eye and the atmosphere. It follows his established method of defining the physical requirements for sight before moving to specific observations.</meta>

# Smoke

First, the manner of seeing shall be defined <note>original: "deſonito," likely "definito"</note>, so that through it one may recognize why light is necessary for such an effect, and especially within the air through which it passes.

Furthermore, it is necessary to see the manner in which smoke presents itself; and whether, through sight, one recognizes how similar smokes are converted into darkness during the destruction of matter—specifically smoke within flame, or smoke within mist. Because these clouds of smoke arise, one must consider the smoke that rises into the air: clouds must follow. Therefore, the true conclusion to be known and seen is how all the objects presented are seen differently; it follows <note>original: "conciene," likely "conviene," meaning it is necessary or follows logically</note> that no single appearance of seeing is the absolute truth.

-> A great appearance of seeing is necessary. <-

<note>Leonardo is exploring the subjective nature of vision. He argues that because smoke and clouds are constantly changing state—from flame to soot to mist—our eyes perceive them differently depending on the light and the medium (the air). Therefore, there is no single "true" way an object looks; its appearance is entirely dependent on its environment.</note>

<summary>Leonardo defines the necessity of light for vision and investigates how smoke, mist, and clouds change their appearance as they rise and dissipate in the air. He concludes that visual perception is variable and dependent on environmental conditions rather than representing a single, fixed truth.</summary>

<keywords>optics, atmospheric perspective, smoke, light, vision, clouds, perception, physics of air</keywords>

# f. 71

->The wheel of motion does not always lose its proportion during the motion of impetus.<- <note>The phrase *moto d'intenzione* refers to "impetus"—the force or momentum a body possesses after the initial moving force has ceased.</note>

<note>The diagram depicts a geometric study of motion, showing a circle (representing a wheel or ball) on a horizontal plane. A series of lines and labels (P, C, A, B, M, N, O, R, F) track the path and state of the object as it moves across various intervals.</note>

<margin>
If it consists of the same weight and value or motions, then every part of the impetus shall always be proportional to the motion performed.

Heavy bodies <term>graui (heavy bodies/weights)</term>: in Renaissance physics, objects that possess gravity and naturally move toward the center of the earth. will move by that motion such that the outcome of said motion shall be proportional to the weight of said motion; or rather, that the weight shall be proportional to the motion performed.
</margin>

If one and the same force were the mover of a ball made of one and the same material, the motion in a portion of its movement would not always be proportional to the motion it will perform in that part; however, it would result as proportional if the motions were always the same, or derived from them.

The reason for this is that it does not always lose a fixed value of speed, but loses it in proportion; and it does not always lose the same weight, but loses it in proportion.

It shall be proportional **m m n n p s r f o m n r f r m p** <note>These letters refer to specific points or intervals marked on the accompanying geometric diagram.</note> which is the weight of the motion of said ball of one and the same material <note>original: "m̃ta" (materia)</note>.

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the physics of a rolling sphere, arguing that the loss of impetus (momentum) and the resulting motion are governed by mathematical proportions rather than constant values. He uses a geometric diagram to map how weight and force interact over the course of an object's movement.</summary>

<keywords>mechanics, impetus, momentum, proportional motion, ballistics, geometry, physics</keywords>

<meta>This page, folio 72r of the Codex Forster II, continues Leonardo's investigations into the geometry of motion. While the previous page (referenced as folio 88r in the records) dealt with the practical application of mechanics and units of weight like ounces and grains, Leonardo here returns to the theoretical foundations of spatial movement—specifically defining the shortest path between two points.</meta>

### Visual Elements

<note>Two geometric diagrams are positioned at the top of the folio, illustrating the concepts discussed in the text.</note>

- **Diagram 1 (left):** A simple circle, likely representing a sphere or a circular path, used as a reference for geometric motion.
- **Diagram 2 (right):** A rectangular prism or channel shown in perspective. Inside the right end of this channel, a circle is inscribed with hatching at its base, possibly representing a cylinder or sphere within a constrained space or a study of perspective and shadows.

---

Whoever must [proceed] by the **straightness** <note>original: "riga", meaning a rule, a straight edge, or the quality of being straight</note> of this returning line
to the direct path of a straight [line] is not forced to be there;
for if a body is moved between two **endpoints**, <term>endpoints (termini)</term>
that line between the said endpoints shall be called **"short"** <note>Leonardo is expressing the Euclidean principle that a straight line is the shortest distance between two points.</note>
the straightness of this line [exists] if the said **motion** is
made between said endpoints according to this rule;
and if the said motion is made along a **curved line**, <note>original: "linia torta"</note>
that which follows said line shall not be called "straight."
Consider again this straightness:
of this straightness which shall be defined by the line;
and if the said motion is made along a **mixed line**, <note>A "mixed line" (linia mista) is a path composed of both straight and curved segments.</note>

of the said line.

# Studies on the Mechanics of Cords and Cylinders

These cylinders are to be understood as such:
and they are for the **cord**, one of which
is wound with a single cord, and the other
with a double cord, as you see here below.

<note>Four drawings of cylinders with varying rope windings, numbered 1 to 4.</note>

And let one of these cords be of triple thickness
compared to the cord of one of these cylinders; and you will see
that the one which is triple the size of one of those
wound on the first cylinder will be
much **slower**. <note>Leonardo uses "tarda" (slow) to describe the increased resistance or the reduced distance traveled per rotation when using thicker ropes.</note>

Furthermore, consider if one of these cords is wound
with a single layer, and the other with a double or triple
layer in sequence. <note>Leonardo is investigating how stacking layers of rope on a drum changes the effective diameter of the cylinder, thereby altering the mechanical advantage.</note>

<column-break/>

<note>The following terms are key to understanding Leonardo's mechanical vocabulary on this page:</note>

*   **Cylinders** <note>original: "cilindri"</note> — The drums or rollers used in winches.
*   **Cord** <note>original: "chorda"</note> — The rope or cable used to transmit force.
*   **Wound** <note>original: "avviluppato"</note> — Wrapped around the drum.
*   **Triple thickness** <note>original: "triplice grosseza"</note> — A rope three times the diameter of the standard one.
*   **Slower** <note>original: "tarda"</note> — Referring to the speed of movement or the increased effort required.
*   **Layer** <note>original: "ordine"</note> — A single complete wrapping of rope covering the surface of the cylinder.

<summary>Leonardo investigates how the thickness of a rope and the number of layers wound around a cylinder affect mechanical speed and resistance. He notes that a triple-thickness cord moves significantly slower than a single one.</summary>

<keywords>mechanics, cylinders, cordage, friction, winding, physics, engineering, rope thickness</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the general mechanical principles of cords and cylinders discussed previously to a specific hydraulic application: the Archimedean screw. Leonardo is now applying his understanding of motion and timing to the movement of water through helical channels.</meta>

# Studies on the Water Screw and Helical Motion

<note>The following text is a series of observations on the timing and synchronization of water as it moves through the multiple internal channels of an Archimedean screw, as seen in the accompanying diagrams.</note>

On one side, the water **ascends** <note>original: "mōti", an abbreviation for "monti" (rises/ascents)</note> through part of its channel, etc., and the other part lowers its [channel]; [at points] **n** and **g**, the water of one ascent waits <note>original: "aspetta"; in this context, it refers to the mechanical delay or the phase shift between the different threads of the screw</note> for the other; ascents of one part, of one ascent, the part waits.

---

One part and the other; ascent, part, its ascent; the ascent of one part waits for the ascent of the other part; and with its ascent, the other part waits for an ascent; ascents of one part wait for the other ascent; from one ascent, the other part waits.

---

And if the water, through its ascent, waits for one part; the ascent of the other part waits; from one ascent, the **part-movement** <note>original: "p. mōte"</note> waits; the part-ascent, the other part waits; and the ascent of one part waits for the other; from one ascent, the other ascent waits for one part; and for one part it waits, the ascent, the other waits; and if the water of one ascent waits for the other ascent; ascents of one part wait for the movement, the other ascent; and the ascent of one part waits for the movement of the other part; ascents of one part wait for the movement of the other part; and for one part it waits, the movement of the other part; ascents of one part wait for the movement of the other part.

<note>Leonardo's repetitive phrasing here mimics the cyclical, rhythmic nature of the screw's rotation. He is tracing the logic of how water in a four-threaded screw (as seen in the first diagram) is distributed. When one "part" or thread of the screw is at its peak, the next thread is "waiting" in a specific position to continue the lift.</note>

<note>The diagrams to the right show different configurations of the **Archimedean screw** <term>Archimedean screw: a machine historically used for transferring water from a low-lying body of water into irrigation ditches</term>. Leonardo's sketches explore different "pitches" (the angle of the threads) and the number of internal channels, which affect the efficiency and speed of the water lift.</note>

<summary>This page explores the sequential logic and timing of water movement within a multi-threaded Archimedean screw. Leonardo analyzes how the water in different channels "waits" or synchronizes during the rotation of the machine.</summary>

<keywords>hydraulics, Archimedean screw, fluid mechanics, helical motion, synchronization, water lift, mechanical engineering</keywords>

# On circular or angular motion

The weight that descends through the air moves so much more slowly as it occupies more air, because the air pushes back against it. <note>Leonardo is observing the principle of air resistance or drag, noting that surface area affects the speed of a falling object.</note>

---

If .d.s.f. were of gold, of silver, of iron, or of lead, and .d.r.g. were of feather or of straw, which weighs as much at .d.f. as .d.g., one must not say that .d.f. would have equal motions <note>original: "moti equali"</note> by your will. If .d.f. were as heavy as if .d.f. were of gold, and you wanted the feather and .d.f. of gold not to be of a single weight <note>original: "graueza"</note> but that it were gold and iron, and .p.f. gold and more; because then the air would act upon .r.s.p.f. of gold and feather as much as the air would not act upon .d.s.f. [which is] purely of gold alone. <note>Leonardo is debating the relationship between density, mass, and air resistance, arguing that two objects of the same weight but different materials (and thus different volumes) will not fall at the same speed.</note>

---

The further the arc is from its center, the slower it is, because the air is broken with more difficulty where the air is more dense. And therefore, if the air were of a single density, .d.f. and .re.s.f.p. would require as much as .p.f.s.r.

---

<note>The following paragraph discusses air resistance and proportions of weight (pondo) in motion.</note>
As is the proportion of the weight <term>pondo: a Latinate term for weight or mass</term> of the feather to the weight of gold, so is the air that it occupies to the weight of the air that it would occupy. And .d.s.f. would not be of such a condition, because the air is broken with more difficulty by a motion in one part than the air of a motion in another, because it pulls the air behind itself in breaking it; the air would not break as much as it would do... it would not break .k.

<note>Annotations on top diagram: 1/2, 3, p, q, 5, f, m, u, x, y</note>
<note>Annotations on middle diagram: m, n, x, r, z, o, k</note>

<vocab>circular motion, angular, weight, feather, air, heaviness, proportion</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo investigates the physics of air resistance, arguing that the volume and material density of an object—not just its weight—determine its speed of descent. He uses geometric diagrams to analyze how air is "broken" or displaced by moving bodies.</summary>
<keywords>aerodynamics, air resistance, gravity, density, circular motion, mechanics, physics</keywords>

--- Page 173 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with a repetitive, meditative exploration of optics and "ocular bodies." This page shifts from the behavior of light to the fundamental principles of statics and gravity, specifically how weight is measured and distributed when suspended or reshaped.</meta>

# On Gravity and Weights

The heavy bodies <note>original: "gravezze"; Leonardo uses this term to describe the quality of heaviness or the objects themselves</note> that pull along a perpendicular line are called weights.

<note>Drawing of a 500 lb rectangular weight suspended by two lines</note>

Equal heavy bodies pulled along a perpendicular line create those same weights as if they were placed one on top of the other. They weigh according to the sum <note>original: "sōma"; a contraction of 'somma'</note> of their weight.

<note>Drawing of a 500 lb wheeled cart suspended by two lines</note>

<margin>
All weights that hang do not lose their weight through the sum of the weight of the perpendiculars. <note>Leonardo is noting that the total weight remains constant regardless of how the suspension lines are arranged, provided they are vertical.</note>
</margin>

Because if you remove one of the weights, the two remaining weights weigh double what remains <note>Leonardo likely means they weigh double the individual unit, or is exploring the ratio of the remaining mass</note>; and if they were all removed, the arm of the balance <term>bilancia: a traditional weighing scale with two hanging pans</term> would not move, being of equal weights. But if one were heavier than the other, one of the ends of the balance would go to the opposite of the heavier part of the balance.

Furthermore, if you weigh a ball of lead with the balance, it will create a certain heaviness for you; and if you make it flat and thin like a leaf of paper, it would not have any more weight than it had before. <note>A fundamental observation in physics: an object's mass and weight remain constant regardless of its shape (deformation).</note>

<note>Vocabulary: heaviness/weights, perpendicular, weights, balance/scale, lead, sum/total</note>

<summary>Leonardo investigates the nature of gravity and weight distribution, concluding that the total weight of an object remains constant regardless of its physical shape or whether it is suspended as a single mass or multiple parts.</summary>
<keywords>statics, gravity, weight distribution, balance scales, lead, physics of shape, perpendicular force</keywords>

--- Page 174 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo's investigation into the physics of weight and gravity. While the previous page focused on solid weights (lead) and their suspension on scales, this page transitions into fluid dynamics, specifically the weight of air and water when measured within their own medium.</meta>

## On the Weight of Air

One first performs the experiment with air, and then in the company of water. Observe whether the bag <note>original: "sacho"; likely a leather bladder or similar flexible container used for containing air underwater</note> inflated under the water produces the same effect in air as it does in water. And if you find that the weight mentioned above rises—whether it be once, twice, three times, or half as much, or whether there is more or less air within the sphere—then you will have successfully proven that air has weight.

You can see this even more clearly if you were to weigh a thin, empty glass sphere <note>original: "palla de uetro"; Leonardo frequently used glass vessels for vacuum and pressure experiments</note>, and then weigh it again with great diligence when it is full of air. This air should be forced in by means of a pump <term>tromba: a compression device, similar to a bellows or a syringe, used to force air into a container</term> placed inside the sphere. You will see that in this sphere, the weight <note>original: "graueza"; heaviness or mass</note> has increased in comparison to the first measurement.

Thus, it is proven that air has weight within air, just as water does within water, and earth within earth; indeed, every heavy body weighs within its own medium.

<summary>Leonardo describes an experiment using an inflated bladder and a glass sphere to prove that air possesses weight even when surrounded by air, establishing a universal principle for all elements.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, weight of air, gravity, atmospheric pressure, glass sphere experiment, physics of elements</keywords>

--- Page 175 ---

Here, he moves from fluid dynamics to the study of statics and the inclined plane, exploring how weight and motion are proportional in mechanical systems.</meta>

# Study of the Perpetual Motion Wheel

<note>Leonardo is investigating a "perpetual motion" wheel. He analyzes the movement of weights on articulated arms, ultimately concluding that such a device cannot provide continuous motion because the center of gravity will always settle at the lowest point, reaching a state of equilibrium.</note>

<note>The diagram above shows a wheel with twelve articulated arms. As the wheel turns, the weights on the left extend outward while those on the right fold inward. Leonardo uses the labels 'm' for a specific weight and 'n' for a hinge to track their path.</note>

Whatever weight is applied to the wheel, which weight is the cause of the motion of said wheel, without a doubt the center of such a weight will come to rest directly below the center of the <term>pole</term><note>The "pole" refers to the central axis or pivot point of the wheel.</note> of said wheel; and no motion will be possible to move it from this position unless additional weight is added to it.

Furthermore, if you had a weight which at every point exited from its wheel and then returned and entered into that wheel, that [weight] would also come to rest between the pole and the <term>lower horizon</term><note>The "lower horizon" refers to the lowest vertical point of the wheel's rotation, or the 6 o'clock position.</note> and would never move by any motion of such a wheel unless a new weight is added.

<note>original: "perpetuum mobile" (perpetual motion), "rota" (wheel), "cientro" (center), "polo" (pole/axis), "peso" (weight), "moto" (motion), "rizzonte" (horizon/level line)</note>

<summary>Leonardo demonstrates through geometry and physics that a "perpetual motion" wheel is impossible because the weights will inevitably seek a point of equilibrium directly below the central axis. He argues that the system will always come to rest unless external force or weight is continuously added.</summary>

<keywords>mechanics, perpetual motion, gravity, equilibrium, physics, center of gravity, Forster Codices, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the overbalanced wheel from the previous page, moving from a twelve-armed design to a wheel with eight curved internal channels containing balls.</meta>

# Study of the Overbalanced Wheel with Eight Channels

<note>Drawing of a wheel with eight curved internal channels containing balls, a study for a perpetual motion machine (overbalanced wheel).</note>

It says here that 8 balls <note>original: "balotte"</note> in 8 channels <note>original: "chanali"</note> are to be provided, and there are 4 on one side and 4 on the other, opposite to each other. Because it says that those which depart from **m** are 4, and they run far from the diameter <note>original: "diamitro"</note>, and those 2 which are **n**, **n**, **m**, because they are from **n** on the opposite side, they are **d**, one for the other, in such a way that one pulls for the other **m**, and the other **m**.

But the ball rises toward the center of the wheel <note>original: "rota"</note>, which it must not do, not giving them **m**, **m** by the force of the **s** counterweight <note>original: "contra peso"</note>, which runs weakly **m**, **n**, **m**. The force of that which he says is far from the center of **m**, therefore it will not create motion <note>original: "moto"</note>; and all the other balls will perform the effect that they ought to perform.

<note>Leonardo is critiquing a common design for a perpetual motion machine. He observes that while some balls move outward to create leverage, others move toward the center (the axis), which counteracts the intended force. He concludes that the "weak" movement of the counterweights fails to overcome the state of equilibrium, meaning the wheel will eventually stop.</note>

<summary>Leonardo analyzes a wheel design using eight balls in curved channels, concluding that the machine will fail to achieve perpetual motion because the weights moving toward the center neutralize the force of those moving outward.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, perpetual motion, overbalanced wheel, counterweight, equilibrium, Leonardo da Vinci, physics</keywords>

# The Geometry of Water Flow and Terrestrial Centers

<note>The diagram at the top of the page depicts a wheel with twelve spiral arms. Leonardo uses this as a model to explain how water from various mountain "branches" (tributaries) converges toward a single exit point or center.</note>

<note>Leonardo is conceptualizing the Earth's surface as a geometric system. He views mountain ranges and their corresponding valleys as "branches" (tributaries) that funnel water toward a central point of exit, much like the spiral channels in his wheel diagram funnel weights toward the center. This reflects his belief in the "microcosm," where the laws of mechanics and the laws of nature (hydrology) operate under the same geometric principles.</note>

<summary>Leonardo uses a spiral-armed wheel diagram to model how water from various mountain branches and valleys converges at a central exit point, applying geometric principles to terrestrial hydrology.</summary>

# On the Equilibrium of Levers

<note>Study of levers and mechanical equilibrium. Two diagrams at the top show levers with weights at varying distances from a central fulcrum.</note>

<note>Top diagram: A horizontal lever with a fulcrum labeled '8'. At the left end (point 'm'), a weight marked with '+' is suspended. On the right end (point 'A'), there is another '+' above the lever, and a secondary weight marked '+ 1' suspended below it, labeled 'HANDLE . 10' <note>original: "MANIVRA", likely a variant of "manovra" or "manubrio," referring to a handle or a point of manual operation.</note>.</note>

<note>Bottom diagram: A horizontal lever with a fulcrum labeled '8'. On the left lever arm (point 'm'), a weight with '+' is suspended. On the right lever arm (point 'd'), a weight marked '+ 1' is placed on top of the lever.</note>

Whenever two weights counter-balance each other on a <term>lever</term> <note>original: "leua"; a simple machine consisting of a beam pivoted at a fixed hinge, or fulcrum.</note>,
when one of them <insert>is moved</insert> <note>Leonardo corrected "should move" to "is moved"</note>, the other does not move unless
the <term>amplitude</term> <note>original: "anpreza" (ampiezza); the extent or width of the movement.</note> of their motions carries the <term>ratio</term> <note>original: "ragione"; the mathematical relationship or proportion.</note>
of their motions: if 8 to m be the <term>half</term> <note>original: "mità" (metà).</note> of what the <term>handle</term> <note>original: "manura" (manovra); likely referring to the distance of the operating arm.</note> is,
by the ratio of 8 to d. If v to n be the half of what
d to m is, and likewise + 1 be the half of d to 10,
therefore one of the motions is the half of the other. <note>Leonardo is describing the Law of the Lever: for the system to remain in equilibrium during movement, the distance traveled by each weight must be inversely proportional to the weight itself. If one arm is twice as long, its end moves twice as far, and thus requires only half the weight to balance the other side.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains the principle of virtual work and displacement in levers, noting that the ratio of the distances moved by the weights must correspond to the ratio of their distances from the fulcrum to maintain equilibrium.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, lever, equilibrium, ratio, motion, fulcrum, weights, Leonardo da Vinci, physics</keywords>

--- Page 189 ---
<meta>This page shifts from the mechanical equilibrium of levers discussed on the previous page to a natural philosophy investigation of heat, light, and air. It explores how the "virtue" or power of the sun's rays is affected by the presence or absence of wind and the density of the air.</meta>

# On the Power of Solar Rays and the Air

Because <term>virtue</term> <note>original: "uirtu"; in this context, it refers to the inherent power, energy, or physical efficacy of a natural force.</note> is so much stronger the more it is united. This same virtue is in the rays of the sun that strike the earth and render the air as does a <del>fire</del> **steel mirror**. Likewise, as the air is cooled by the virtue of the **wind** within those same rays of the sun, the virtue of that wind joins with the virtue of the sun; by cooling it, it creates cooling rays. And so, conversely, the air heats up in the rays of the sun when it is without wind, because it is more <term>viscous</term> <note>original: "uiscosa"; here meaning dense or stagnant, lacking the thinning effect of motion.</note> and takes on a <term>point</term> <note>original: "pūta" (punta); likely referring to the "sharpness" or intensity of the heat's penetration.</note> and reflects it.

Because the heated air joins with the virtue of the sun and creates very hot rays, conditioned by the motion that it takes toward the heights; it renders the air more viscous and <term>piercing</term> <note>original: "pūtiua" (puntiva); describing the stinging or sharp quality of intense heat.</note>. Whence the air without wind is hotter; and if it is without upward motion, it is more cooling because it has hot rays that are less piercing.

<note>The author is attempting to explain the thermodynamics of the atmosphere, suggesting that wind can "unite" with solar rays to change their quality, and that stagnant air allows heat to become more concentrated or "sharp."</note>

<summary>This page explores the physics of heat and light, arguing that the intensity of the sun's "virtue" depends on whether the air is moving or stagnant. It posits that wind can modify the quality of solar rays, while still air becomes "viscous" and allows heat to become more piercing.</summary>
<keywords>virtue, solar rays, atmospheric heat, wind, viscosity, optics, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 192 ---
<meta>Continuing from the study of levers and the mechanical balance on the previous page (f. 3r), Leonardo now applies these principles of equilibrium to create a scientific instrument for measuring the natural environment—specifically, the moisture content of the air.</meta>

# f. 3v

<note>This diagram illustrates one of Leonardo’s most famous inventions: the hygrometer. The device consists of a horizontal beam or axle. On the left, two cords wrap around the axle and pass over pulleys labeled 's' and 'p'. These cords support two suspended square containers. The 's' stands for *spugna* (sponge) and the 'p' for *peso* (weight). On the right, a crank handle allows for the adjustment or resetting of the mechanism.</note>

This is the way to weigh the air: with this instrument, one will be able to know the difference in the weight of a quantity of air from one time <note>Leonardo uses a small cross inside a circle here as a reference mark to denote a specific instance or measurement.</note> to another, and in how short an amount of time such weight changes in the same place.

<note>Leonardo’s "way to weigh the air" is essentially a moisture meter. He realized that a dry sponge would absorb water from the atmosphere as humidity increased, becoming heavier. By balancing a sponge against a fixed weight on a scale, he could visually track the changes in atmospheric density and moisture. While we now know that humid air is actually less dense than dry air, Leonardo’s device correctly measured the increase in the *weight* of the absorbing material (the sponge) relative to the surrounding environment.</note>

<summary>Leonardo describes and illustrates his invention of a hygrometer, an instrument designed to measure changes in atmospheric humidity by weighing a sponge against a counterweight. This represents an early attempt to quantify meteorological phenomena using mechanical principles.</summary>

<keywords>hygrometer, atmospheric pressure, humidity, meteorology, measurement, weight of air, scientific instruments, sponge, equilibrium</keywords>

# On the Mechanics of the Balance and Oblique Forces

<note>The following diagrams and text analyze the mechanics of a balance where weights are suspended at an angle (obliquely) rather than vertically.</note>

<note>Upper diagram: A horizontal lever arm divided into five segments, with labels **t**, **f**, **p**, **d**, and **s**. Two weights are suspended by cords that converge or angle toward specific points on the arm.</note>

<note>Lower diagram: A similar study with a lever arm divided into six segments, labeled **o**, **n**, **m**, and **s**.</note>

<margin>
balancing <note>original: "libramēto"</note>
</margin>

->The weight that hangs from the **balance** <note>original: "bilācier"</note> at an angle, [explained] so briefly.<-

---

The force of the **power** <term>potētia: the effort or counter-force required to maintain equilibrium</term> will be less than the weight hanging from the **scale** <note>original: "libra"</note> at an angle, in the same proportion that the distance between the **line of motion** <note>Leonardo uses the "line of motion" to describe the vertical path of a weight, which defines its effective leverage or "potential arm."</note> of the weight is shorter than the line of the power that supports it—which is not the physical **straight arm** <note>original: "dritto braccio"</note> of the scale at points **f**, **p**, and **s**.

<summary>Leonardo investigates the physics of equilibrium, demonstrating how a weight's effective force is reduced when suspended at an angle. He establishes that the true lever arm is the horizontal distance to the weight's vertical line of action, not the physical length of the balance beam.</summary>

<keywords>balance, equilibrium, oblique force, lever arm, line of motion, mechanics, statics, potential arm</keywords>

# The Harmonics

<note>The title "li armoniaci" likely refers to the "harmonics" or proportional relationships of weights and measures in a mechanical system.</note>

->[Diagram showing a quadrant of a circle with a vertical arm (n-d) and a horizontal arm (n-a). A slanted arm (n-m) extends through the arc to point 87. A weight (g) hangs from the pivot point (n). The point (p) on the horizontal arm represents the vertical projection of (m).]<-

The point of every <term>n-m-d</term><note>The path of the lever arm as it moves through the arc.</note> and the other of the point of <term>n-p</term><note>The horizontal distance from the center to the vertical drop-line of the weight.</note>.
As long as <term>n-m-d</term> is, and <term>n-d</term> is as much as the arm <term>n-p</term>,
so much shall be the weight of the one arm <term>n-m</term>.
That the weight <term>n-p</term> shall be of <term>n-m</term> the weight, the
weight as much as shall be the weight of <term>n-m</term>, so much
with every arm <term>n-p</term> through that same arm.

<note>Leonardo is describing the principle of the "potential lever arm." He is observing that the mechanical advantage (or "weight") of the slanted arm (n-m) is not determined by its actual length, but by its horizontal distance (n-p) from the central axis (n-d). The further the arm moves toward the horizontal (n-a), the greater its "moment" or effective force becomes.</note>

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the mechanical moment of a slanted lever arm, demonstrating that its effective force is proportional to its horizontal projection (the potential lever) rather than its physical length. This geometric study is a foundational step toward understanding the laws of static equilibrium and the center of gravity.</summary>

<keywords>mechanics, potential lever, moment of force, geometry, Leonardo da Vinci, physics, equilibrium, weights</keywords>

<meta>The previous page concluded with a technical study of air displacement and mechanical levers. This page shifts abruptly from physics to a moral and psychological observation regarding the character of the artist or intellectual, specifically addressing the relationship between lack of talent and the tendency to criticize others.</meta>

He who is of less <term>wit<note>original: "ingiegnio" (ingegno); refers to innate talent, creative intelligence, or technical skill.</note></term> will become more presumptuous, and will be lacking in wit.

---

The man who is of little worth will always be presumptuous and resentful, and always finds fault in others. Lacking in wit, he always strives to be seen as one who finds fault in others; and the more he lacks in wit and goodness, the greater will be the habit of finding fault. Whenever he seeks to condemn the work of others, he shows a lack of wit in proportion to the fault he finds where none exists in the work of others; and so great will be his lack of wit.

<note>Leonardo reflects on the psychology of the critic. He suggests that the act of "finding fault" (manchamento) in another's work is actually a mirror of the critic's own "lack" (manchamento) of talent. The more one condemns (dāpnare) without cause, the more they reveal their own intellectual poverty.</note>

<summary>A moral aphorism by Leonardo da Vinci observing that individuals with the least talent or "wit" are often the most arrogant and critical of others' accomplishments. He argues that unfounded criticism is a definitive sign of the critic's own intellectual and moral deficiency.</summary>
<keywords>wit, talent, presumption, criticism, psychology, ethics, artistic character</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the mechanics of levers and balances. While the previous page focused on the angle of ropes and the "goal" of motion in pendulums, this page shifts to the mathematical calculation of "moments"—the relationship between weight and distance from the fulcrum.</meta>

<note>Diagram of a simple lever with a central fulcrum and weights of varying values suspended at different distances.</note>

If 10 pounds <note>original: "lb" or "libra" — a historical unit of weight roughly equivalent to 327 grams</note> weigh down 2 <term>braccia: arms — a Renaissance unit of length based on the human arm, roughly 58-62 cm</term> of a lever, 1 pound of weight exerts the same force at 20 arms of the lever. Therefore, 1 pound at 20 arms of the lever will exert the same force as 2 pounds at 10 arms of the lever, and 4 pounds at 5 arms of the lever. You must reduce <note>original: "ridochere" — to calculate or convert</note> the weights into "lever arms" <note>Leonardo is calculating what we now call the "moment of force" (weight × distance)</note> to understand the function <note>original: "offitio" — the duty or mechanical effect</note> of each one individually in that lever.

<note>A more complex study of a lever system, showing a horizontal beam with multiple suspension points and weights labeled with numerical values (4, 4 on the left; 1, 2, 3 on the right) to calculate moments of force.</note>

<summary>Leonardo demonstrates the law of the lever, explaining that force is determined by the product of weight and its distance from the fulcrum. He provides a mathematical exercise in maintaining equilibrium by adjusting these two variables.</summary>

<keywords>mechanics, lever, equilibrium, weight, distance, physics, statics, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical exploration of the law of the lever. While the previous page established the basic principle that weight multiplied by distance creates a specific "force" or "moment," this page applies that logic to more complex distributions of weights across multiple arms of a balance.</meta>

<note>Study of equilibrium and levers with suspended weights. The diagrams illustrate the law of the lever, specifically how unequal weights can balance one another at different distances from a fulcrum.</note>

<note>Simple lever diagram: A horizontal beam balanced on a central point. A weight of 8 is on the left; weights of 4, 2, and 1 are distributed on the right.</note>

The ratio <note>original: "ragio." for "ragione"</note> of weights suspended at various arms <note>The "arms" are the sections of the lever extending from the center</note> and of as many <term>braccia: arms — a unit of length</term> as the arms are, so that the weights are matched with the arms of the other rod <note>original: "asta"</note> of the weights. For their arms, divide the weights in such a way that the weight of 8 is matched with the other part of that arm, just as the arms of half a <term>braccia</term> each do; because the arms are in opposition <note>original: "i' d'opposito"</note> to one another, the weights [balance] each other by their arms.

---

<note>Compound lever diagram showing a hierarchical distribution of weights. A primary lever supports secondary levers, distributing the load of weights 4, 2, and 1 to balance the single weight of 8 on the opposite side.</note>

<note>Weights in the diagram are labeled 8, 4, 2, and 1 in standard Arabic numerals.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explores the proportional relationship between weights and their distances from a fulcrum, demonstrating how a single large weight can be balanced by several smaller weights distributed along a lever. He concludes that for equilibrium to exist, the weights must be inversely proportional to the lengths of the lever arms.</summary>

# Folio 43r

<note>Top left block of text</note>

These moving bodies <note>original: "corpi mossi"</note>
you see that they change by measure
because each one has its own proportion <note>original: "p" used as an abbreviation for "proporzione"</note>
one hour, every four proportions for one
while the other changes one
time, which is by measure of a
fifth of an hour.

---

<note>Geometric diagram study of a quadrant</note>
<note>The diagram depicts a quarter-circle (quadrant) within a square frame. Five radial lines, labeled 0 through 4, extend from the bottom-left corner. Small circular weights or "bodies" are positioned along the curved arc where the lines intersect it, illustrating the division of a movement into segments.</note>

---

<note>Main text block below the diagram</note>

You see that if they move through a space of
four hours, as shown by 0, 1, 2, 3, 4,
this one, which by the measure of one hour,
moves the first by the whole, the second by half,
the other by a quarter, the other by an eighth,
the other by a sixteenth, the other by a thirty-
second part of an hour. <note>Leonardo is describing a geometric progression where each successive movement covers half the distance or time of the previous one.</note>

<summary>Leonardo uses a geometric quadrant diagram to illustrate the proportional division of time and motion, demonstrating how a single duration can be subdivided into a series of halves (1/2, 1/4, 1/8, etc.). This study reflects his interest in the mathematical relationship between space traveled and time elapsed.</summary>

<note>The following text is transcribed from Leonardo's mirror writing, which reads right-to-left. It explores principles of equilibrium and the center of gravity using levers and weights.</note>

<note>Top diagram: A balance beam suspended from a central point. A weight labeled "f" hangs from the left arm, and a larger weight labeled "f" with a cross (+) hangs from the right arm. The fulcrum is positioned closer to the heavier weight to demonstrate equilibrium.</note>

<note>Middle diagram: A horizontal beam divided into segments, with labels "n", "s", "m", and "n" at various points.</note>

<note>Bottom diagram: A beam labeled at points "p", "d", and "s". A weight labeled "f" hangs from the left end (p). A mark "+" is placed below the center point (d).</note>

Make it so that one *f* is a weight of 1 <term>lb</term><note>original: "lb" (libra); a pound, the standard unit of weight.</note> and the other *f* is 4 lbs. Let the greater weight be 4 times closer to the <term>center</term><note>original: "cientro"; here referring to the fulcrum or pivot point of the balance.</note> than the smaller one, and there will be <term>equality</term><note>original: "parita"; a state of balance or equilibrium.</note>. If the weight of 4 lbs were at *p*, it would still be equal to the 1 lb at *f*. And as one is in the middle of the half, and the other from one end to the other end, it shall be of the same <term>proportion</term><note>original: "ragione"; literally 'reason', but used here in the mathematical sense of ratio or proportion.</note>, and the same will happen with the center of <term>gravity</term><note>original: "graueza"; heaviness or gravity.</note>.

<summary>Leonardo demonstrates the law of the lever, explaining that equilibrium is achieved when the distance from the fulcrum is inversely proportional to the weight. He specifically illustrates how a 4-pound weight can balance a 1-pound weight by being placed four times closer to the center.</summary>

<keywords>mechanics, equilibrium, center of gravity, lever, balance, proportion, physics, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

# [Folio 116r]

<note>Study of mechanical equilibrium and the center of gravity of a balance beam. Leonardo explores how the weight of the beam itself, when added to external weights, affects the point of balance. The text is written in Leonardo's characteristic right-to-left mirror script.</note>

<note>Top diagram: A balance beam with weights at its ends and points marked along its length. Weights are labeled '6' and '3'. Points on the bar are labeled 'n' (left end), 'm' (right end), and 'c', 'd', 'a' near the center. Numbers '8', '12', and '4' are written below the central portion of the bar.</note>

Let . a . be the center <note>the fulcrum or pivot point</note> of the balance . and . b . shall be the middle of its length . let . n . m . be its other length . which shall be . 3 . and . 6 . <note>referring to the proportional distances from the pivot</note> I say that if the weight <note>original: "graueza," literally heaviness or gravity</note> placed at the end . n . is . 4 . the weight placed at the end . m . shall be doubled . and shall be . 8 . and the other is . 4 . the sum <note>original: "sōma"</note> shall be . 12 . which shall be at the middle . d . because the entire weight of the balance will be rested in the middle.

---

<note>Bottom diagram: A similar study of a balance beam with asymmetrical weights '1' and '2'. The bar is marked with points 's', 'v', 'r', with 'v' corresponding to a central graduated scale. This demonstrates Leonardo's interest in the "steelyard" style of balance, where a sliding weight or a graduated scale helps determine the weight of an object.</note>

<note>Leonardo's vocabulary on this page focuses on the fundamentals of statics: the balance (*bilācia*), the weight or gravity of the objects (*graueza*), the midpoint (*mezo*), the total length (*lūgheza*), and the calculation of the total sum (*sōma*) of forces acting on the lever.</note>

<summary>Leonardo calculates the center of gravity for a balance beam, demonstrating how the physical weight of the lever itself must be combined with external weights to find the true point of equilibrium. He uses specific numerical examples to show that for a beam to balance, the sum of the weights and the distances must remain proportional.</summary>

<keywords>balance, equilibrium, center of gravity, mechanics, lever, weight distribution, statics, physics</keywords>

--- Page 235 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion on cooling methods and the Grigna mountains to a fundamental physical principle: the gravity of water and its natural tendency to seek the center of the Earth.</meta>

Note how, when water descends from its source, it moves toward the center of the earth by traveling in a straight line toward that center. This occurs because it is approaching its <term>site of rest</term> <note>original: "sito di riposo"; Leonardo's term for the point of equilibrium where a body no longer feels the urge to move.</note>, for it draws near to that center which is the site of rest for the entire universe.

<note>The diagram shows water flowing from a horizontal channel (labeled 'm') into a rectangular reservoir, which then drains through a curved outlet (labeled 'n'). This illustrates Leonardo's observations on the gravity and natural motion of water towards the center of the Earth.</note>

<margin>
straight line m
comes from above
through the course of the water
made by the said water
because water is a
thing that draws near to the
center of the earth
</margin>

---

For this reason, water descends from a high place to a low place until it finds its site of rest, which is the center of the earth; for this reason, the water comes from above.

<note>Leonardo uses the phrase "center of the earth" (centro della terra) to describe the gravitational pull that dictates the behavior of all fluids and heavy bodies.</note>

<summary>A scientific study on the mechanics of water flow between connected vessels. Leonardo concludes that a vessel's water level remains constant when the rate of influx from a source equals the rate of discharge through its outlet.</summary>

# On the Movement of Weights

<note>The following text is written in Leonardo's characteristic mirror writing. It is a dense, repetitive analysis of how specific points of weight (labeled n, m, and q) shift their positions relative to a central axis.</note>

If you pull the three **heavy bodies** <note>original: "gravi"; refers to weights or masses in a mechanical system</note> at an equal distance from the center of the world <note>original: "ciētro del modo"; in Leonardo's physics, this usually refers to the center of gravity or the Earth's center</note>, they will come to take up... <note>The text here is highly abbreviated and repetitive, reflecting a draft of a mathematical proof.</note>

Take **n**; they do those which **o** and **q** will become at points **m** and **d**, lenticular... at points **n**, **m**, and **q**. Because if you placed points **n**, **m**, and **q** at **p**, **s**, **d**, and **q**, to make **e** and **x** of **n**, **m**, and **q**, point **o** of **d**, **q**, **b**, **s**, **d**, and **q** will result. And the points **n**, **m**, and **q** will have come into points **b**, **s**, **d**, and **q**. Points **n**, **m**, and **q** will be able to make points **n**, **m**, and **q** come into point **d**. And points **n**, **m**, and **q** will result in making **s**, **s**... the **power** <note>original: "virtu"; refers to the mechanical force or potential energy</note> of **m** will be...

The points **n**, **m**, and **q** will become points **n**, **m**, and **q**; the pointers **n**, **m**, and **q** to make **i**, **b**, **s**, **d**, and **q** in point **n**, **m**, and **q**. Points **n**, **m**, and **q** will become point **m**, **d**, **q**, **s**. The points of **m** and **q** will result; points **o**, **d**, and **q** will result to make point **n**, **m**, and **q** of **o**, **d**, and **q** in point **d**. And points **o**, **m**, and **q** will result in point **o**, **d**, and **q**. Points **n**, **m**, and **q** will result in point **o**, **d**, and **q**. Points **n**, **m**, and **q** will result in making **m**, **o**, **d**, and **q**. Point **n**, **m**, and **q** will result in point **n**, **m**, and **q**. **n** will result in points **n**, **m**, and **q**. Point **o**, **d**, and **q** will result in point **n**, **m**, and **q**. **n** will result in points **n**, **m**, and **q**. Point **o**, **d**, and **q** will result.

<margin>
<note>A large decorative reference mark, possibly used to link this observation to another page or a specific part of the diagram.</note>
</margin>

In the sum of everything, in truth, point **n**, **m**, **q**, **n**... will come to take **n**...

<summary>This page contains a technical geometric analysis of three weights (n, m, q) and their potential movement or equilibrium positions relative to a central point. Leonardo uses a repetitive, almost algebraic shorthand to track how these points of mass would shift within a mechanical system of arcs and vertical lines.</summary>

# On Weights Made by the Power of the Lever
<note>original: "li pesi fatti . p . d . f."; likely an abbreviation for "per della forza" (by means of force/power)</note>

->Weights made by the power of the lever<-

<note>The diagram shows a horizontal balance beam. To the left of the central fulcrum are points f, d, and e; to the right are c, b, and a. Weights are suspended as follows: a weight of 1 at point f (farthest left), a weight of 2 at point d, a weight of 4 at point b, and a weight of 8 at point a (farthest right). This illustrates the inverse relationship between weight and distance from the center.</note>

If one weight is 8 times greater than the other, just as 8 is to 1, the arm of the **balance** <note>original: "balancia"</note> will equilibrate with the other; and by "arm" one understands the arrangement of the weights on either side.

->Proportion of one to another<-

And whatever the distance of these weights may be, one will be greater than the other in the same **proportion** <note>original: "proporcione"</note> as the weights; for example, if **d** were double **f**.

Conversely, one will be greater than the other just as the **distance** <note>original: "distāza"</note> of these weights is from the **center** <note>original: "cientro"</note> of their **common weight** <note>term: common weight (comune peso) — Leonardo's term for the center of gravity or the point of equilibrium where the system balances</note>; for example, if **a** were double **d**.

The part of the weight that is closer to the center will be, in that same order, heavier than the other part which is [further] from the center. <note>Leonardo is describing the "effective weight" or torque; a weight closer to the fulcrum must be heavier to balance a lighter weight further away.</note>

<margin>Because</margin>

<note>The following is a list of technical terms used in the manuscript</note>
**Weights, arm, balance, proportion, distance, center, common weight**

<summary>This page illustrates the law of the lever, demonstrating that for a beam to balance, the weights must be in inverse proportion to their distance from the fulcrum. Leonardo uses a numerical example (1, 2, 4, 8) to show how equilibrium is maintained through geometric progression.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, law of the lever, equilibrium, center of gravity, proportion, Leonardo da Vinci, physics</keywords>

<note>This page is a dense exercise in geometric notation. Leonardo is using the letters on the diagram to create a "shorthand" for his calculations of equilibrium. The "space" (spacio) refers to the distance from the vertical axis, while the weights (pesi) refer to the numbered blocks (1, 2, 4, 5) seen in the illustration. He is essentially writing out the ratios required to keep the circular mechanism in balance.</note>

# Manuscript Page: Studies on Equilibrium

<note>Diagram of a wheel with three spokes. Weights are suspended from the ropes passing over the wheel. Annotations on the weights: 10 and 5 on the left, 4 on the right.</note>

Because the <term>opposing weights</term><note>original: "pesi contrari"; these are weights acting in opposite directions across a balance point.</note> pass through the
line of that <term>center</term><note>original: "centro"; the axis or fulcrum around which the wheel rotates.</note> of the circle, there
still remains the <term>weak weight</term><note>original: "peso debile"; the weight that exerts less force due to its mass or position.</note> against the <term>strong</term><note>original: "forte"; the weight exerting greater force.</note>.
