This same operation you shall perform for the <term>counterweights</term><note>original: "contra pesi"; weights used to offset or balance an existing load.</note>;
furthermore, let the weight be as far <term>removed</term><note>original: "rimosso"; refers to the distance of the weight from the center of rotation.</note>
from the center against the weight, as far
as the weight remains opposite.

<summary>Leonardo explores the mechanical principles of equilibrium using a three-spoked wheel, analyzing how the distance from the center affects the balance between opposing weights. These studies are foundational to his understanding of torque and the behavior of weights in circular motion.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the physics of equilibrium, specifically focusing on how the distance from a fulcrum affects the weight required to maintain balance.</meta>

<note>This folio contains geometric and mechanical studies on the equilibrium of unequal balances and the law of the lever. Leonardo uses circular diagrams and linear scales to analyze weight distribution and distance from the center of rotation.</note>

<note>Upper diagram: A circle with three radii meeting at the center. Below it, a horizontal lever labeled with letters 'd', 'z', 'A' and a secondary scale labeled 'n', 'c'. Symbols include '1', '+', and 'L' (or 'S'). A label 'MAN' <note>likely an abbreviation for 'maniera' or 'manner'</note> appears to the right.</note>

If the unequal <term>balance</term><note>original: "bilancia"; refers here to a weighing scale or lever</note> were of equal weights, it would remain stationary when they weigh as much as they have from the point <note>meaning the distance from the fulcrum or center of gravity</note>.

<note>Middle diagram: A smaller version of the circular study with three radii.</note>

To know why equal weights remain stationary, it is necessary that the weight which weighs more be further from its center <term>d</term><note>the designated point on the diagram</note>; so that it can remain stationary, that which moves further from its center weighs more than that which is near to that center point.

The balance departs from the equal balance <note>meaning it becomes an "unequal" or asymmetrical lever</note> because if all the weights were equal there, they would remain stationary in whatever way it was designed.

<summary>Leonardo explores the fundamental principles of the lever, observing that for an asymmetrical balance to remain in equilibrium, the weight further from the center must exert more force than the one closer to it. He uses circular and linear diagrams to visualize how distance from the fulcrum compensates for differences in weight.</summary>

<keywords>mechanics, law of the lever, equilibrium, balance, geometry, physics, weights, fulcrum</keywords>

---

<term>weight (peso)</term>: The force exerted by a body due to gravity.
<term>gravity (gravità)</term>: The natural tendency of heavy bodies to move toward the center of the Earth.
<term>center of the world (centro del mondo)</term>: In the pre-Copernican worldview, the Earth was the center of the universe, and its center was the destination for all falling objects.
<term>motion (moto)</term>: The act of changing place or position.
<term>rest (dimora)</term>: The state of being stationary or at a fixed point.
<term>pulling (tiran)</term>: The action of drawing or hauling a load.

<summary>Leonardo explores the fundamental principles of gravity, asserting that all weights naturally seek the center of the Earth in a straight line. The accompanying diagrams study the mechanical forces involved in pulling a weight horizontally using a counterweight system.</summary>
<keywords>gravity, mechanics, center of the earth, traction, physics, equilibrium, motion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 124 regarding the behavior of weights and their relationship to the center of the Earth. While the previous page established that all weights seek the "center of the world," this page examines the specific state of a weight that has already reached its point of equilibrium.</meta>

### On the nature of motion

The weight that rests
at the center of its
slow motion does not
move in any
direction, because it
finds no place where it can
become the victor <note>original: "vinçitore". Leonardo uses this term to describe a force that overcomes resistance or an opposing weight to create movement.</note>, because
from whatever side
it rests—it
falls along the
equal line between one
pole and the other,
so that it is balanced
over the center.

<note>The diagram on the right illustrates a balance or pulley system. A circular wheel with center 'o' has weights 'm' suspended from the ends of its horizontal diameter 'd-a'. A horizontal line 'p-q' is tangent to the base of the wheel. Below the weights are series of small circles (marked 'oooooo p'), possibly representing units of force or displacement.</note>

<column-break/>

<note>The following terms are central to Leonardo's mechanical vocabulary on this page.</note>

<term>motion (moto)</term>: The physical displacement of an object. Leonardo distinguishes between "slow" and "natural" motions.
<term>weight (peso)</term>: The downward force of an object.
<term>center (cientro)</term>: Here referring to the center of gravity or the point of equilibrium.
<term>pole (polo)</term>: The endpoints of an axis or diameter; in this context, the opposing points of a balance.
<term>line (linia)</term>: The path of force or gravity.
<term>victor (vinçitore)</term>: The dominant force in a system of weights that causes motion to occur.
<term>nature (natura)</term>: The inherent laws governing the physical world.

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the state of equilibrium, explaining that a weight positioned perfectly at its center of motion remains stationary because its forces are balanced and cannot "overcome" themselves to create movement. This study uses a pulley or wheel diagram to visualize the distribution of mass around a central axis.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, equilibrium, center of gravity, physics of motion, statics, Leonardo da Vinci, weights and measures</keywords>

# motion

If one pulley moves faster 
than the other, while both are of 
the very same thickness <note>original: "grossezza". In Leonardo's mechanical studies, this refers to the diameter or circumference of the pulley wheel.</note>, 
in the first place I say 
that there is no motion, nor does one 
pulley move faster than 
the other [relative to the] rope.

<note>Diagram of a complex pulley system with five wheels and two weights labeled with the number 8. Arrows below the weights indicate downward tension, exploring the distribution of force across multiple points of rotation.</note>

From p. f. joined, the m. n. r. f. have <note>original: "anno", a common Renaissance spelling for "hanno" (they have). This likely refers to labels on a corresponding diagram.</note>

• pulley d. d. n. f. d.
these similar pulleys

<note>Diagram at the bottom right showing a compound pulley block with multiple internal wheels and the intricate threading of the rope, demonstrating how mechanical advantage is multiplied by increasing the number of wheels.</note>

<term>motion (movimento)</term>: The displacement of an object or part of a machine.
<term>pulley (pulega/pulege)</term>: A wheel with a grooved rim around which a cord passes, used to change the direction of a force or to provide mechanical advantage.
<term>thickness (grossezza)</term>: The diameter or circumference of the pulley; Leonardo notes that identical "thickness" ensures identical rotational speed.
<term>place (luocho)</term>: Here used as a rhetorical device ("in the first place") to establish the primary rule of the system.
<term>rope (corda)</term>: The line connecting the weights and pulleys; its uniform movement is the subject of the investigation.

<summary>Leonardo examines the mechanics of pulleys, asserting that wheels of identical diameter must move at the same speed when connected by a single rope. The page features several sketches of complex pulley blocks and a study of a cube's orientation as it moves through a channel.</summary>
<keywords>pulleys, mechanics, motion, mechanical advantage, rope tension, geometry of solids, friction</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion on circular motion and equilibrium to a specific analysis of the "potential" or effective weight of objects moving along inclined (oblique) paths versus vertical ones.</meta>

# 55

<note>The following text is written in Leonardo's characteristic mirror script (right-to-left) and is transcribed here in normal left-to-right reading order, preserving original spelling and abbreviations.</note>

The gravity <note>original: "gravità"; here meaning the effective weight or downward force</note> of weights which
move along an oblique line is greater than that
which moves along a perpendicular line <note>Leonardo is exploring the concept of "accidental" versus "natural" gravity, or how a weight's force is perceived differently depending on its path of descent</note>.

By as much as the weights are further along the arm
of the balance that supports them, so much greater is
the gravity—or we might say its perpendicular weight—
that supports it from that side.

With these two weights, or should one or the other be lacking,
we wish to see what the power <note>original: "potētia"; in this context, the mechanical advantage or force exerted</note> of said
weights might be.

---

<note>The diagram shows a balance mechanism with a horizontal beam and an inclined arm. Leonardo uses these geometric sketches to calculate the "functional" length of a lever arm, which changes as the weight moves through an arc or down a slope.</note>

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the physics of the lever, arguing that the effective force (gravity) of a weight changes based on its distance from the pivot and whether its path is vertical or inclined. This demonstrates his early grasp of the decomposition of forces and mechanical advantage.</summary>
<keywords>gravity, weights, oblique line, perpendicular, balance, lever arm, mechanics, potential force</keywords>

# 127

How the line is made in front of **d** <note>original: "chome fassi la riga dinançi a la d"; Leonardo is describing the geometric construction of a specific line relative to the point labeled 'd' in his diagram. The term "riga" (line) here refers to the calculated path or vector in his study of mechanics.</note>

Drawing <note>original: "disegnio"; referring to the technical sketches and geometric proofs on this page.</note>

---

<note>The text is written in Leonardo's characteristic mirror script. These brief instructions serve as captions for his diagrams. The sketches depict segmented arms meeting at a vertex (point 'd'), a method Leonardo frequently used to analyze the "arms" of a balance and how the distance from a central pivot changes the "potential" or effective weight of an object.</note>

<note>The diagrams represent a tri-radial structure. In the context of his other writings on this subject, the vertical line (topped with 'a') likely represents the line of gravity, while the segmented horizontal and angled arms represent the movement of weights. By dividing these arms into segments, Leonardo can geometrically calculate the shifting center of gravity and mechanical advantage.</note>

<summary>Leonardo provides a brief instruction for a geometric construction related to his diagrams, specifically detailing how to draw a line relative to a central point (d) to analyze mechanical forces.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, technical drawing, mechanics, point d, proportional measurement, perspective, statics</keywords>

of the other part ->the weight of the one<-

<note>Diagram of a horizontal balance beam with a fulcrum marked 's'. The right side (+) is thicker and divided into four segments; the left side (-) is thinner. This illustrates Leonardo's study of how a thicker, heavier arm can be balanced by a longer, thinner one.</note>

The weight of one of the parts of the **balance** multiplied by its distance from the **center** of the balance is equal to the multiplication of the weight of the other part of the balance by its distance from the center of the balance.

...so that there may be parity <note>original: "parità"; here meaning equilibrium or a state of perfect balance</note> between one part and the other; or rather, one of the weights will weigh as much [as the other] by means of the weight of one [side] of the balance.

<note>Diagram of a horizontal balance beam with divisions marked 'M' on the left and a weight 'A' on the right. A vertical line indicates the fulcrum. This sketch represents a more abstract mathematical proof of the law of the lever.</note>

<margin>
of the balance
</margin>
These weights are of such a nature among themselves:

because if the weight of one of the parts of the balance were equal to the weight of the whole balance itself, it would of necessity have to be as far from the center of the balance as is half of the entire balance. <note>Leonardo is calculating the center of gravity of the beam itself. He posits that if a single weight equals the mass of the entire scale, its position relative to the fulcrum must account for the beam's own weight distribution to maintain equilibrium.</note>

<summary>Leonardo defines the fundamental law of the lever, explaining that equilibrium is reached when the product of weight and distance is equal on both sides. He also notes how the weight of the balance beam itself must be factored into these mechanical calculations.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, law of the lever, equilibrium, balance beam, center of gravity, proportional weight, statics, physics</keywords>

<note>Drawing of a rectangular block with a notched or stepped cut, likely a mechanical component or a study of joinery.</note>

<term>catch</term><note>original: "chastagna"; literally 'chestnut', but in a mechanical context, Leonardo often uses this to refer to a pawl, click, or notched component used to stop motion.</note>

in the manner that is

here below: let the small one be suspended
above and the other below: so that
the small one descends and the other rises;
so that one weighs as much as the other in similar motion,
and one will rise as much as the other will descend.

<note>Leonardo is observing that a smaller weight can balance a larger one if the mechanism is arranged so that the smaller weight moves a greater distance. This is an early intuition of the principle of "virtual work"—the idea that weight multiplied by distance moved must remain constant in an ideal system.</note>

# On the Penetration of Light through Humid Bodies

How the rays of the air penetrate into **humid bodies** <note>Leonardo uses "humid bodies" to refer to water, mist, or moisture-laden air</note> without being mixed together at all, so that neither one nor the other would be seen; but it is possible that the humid air, like the **rays of the sun**, may be tinted by some of that humidity in that part:

<column-break/>

From which it arises: if the air were tinted red and were thick enough, it would not appear everywhere as red air in our eyes; but the air and that water mix together and become a blend: if the air were of any color, it would be entirely of the color of that water. <note>Leonardo is exploring the concept of "media" and how the color of a substance (like water or thick mist) influences the perceived color of the light passing through it.</note>

<note>The accompanying diagrams illustrate these principles: the first shows light rays entering a rectangular medium (labeled S and P), while the second shows a ray passing through a circular body (labeled o and m), likely representing a water droplet or a lens to study refraction.</note>

<summary>Leonardo investigates how light rays interact with humid air and water, questioning whether the light takes on the color of the medium it passes through. He uses geometric diagrams to map the path of these rays through different shapes and substances.</summary>

<keywords>optics, light rays, refraction, humidity, atmospheric perspective, color theory, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

<meta>This page shifts from the previous discussion on optics and light to a mechanical and anatomical study of physical force and compression within the human body.</meta>

# On Compression

You shall proceed in this manner within these discourses:
First, we state the definition;
then, we shall reason upon the power <note>original: "potētia" (potentia). In Leonardo’s physics, this refers to "force" or "potential energy" as a physical quantity.</note>,
and whether it is a divine or a natural thing.
In the third place, its birth and
how long it lives. In the fourth way,
the rejoicing of its nature; for if it
were not vain <note>Leonardo often describes force as "vain" or "transient" because it seeks its own destruction through movement and release.</note>, it would never rejoice.
I would say man is of such strength
that he would conquer his enemy,
but being vain, he has not conquered it.
Then, it shall be about the compositions of
our senses, from which this force
is composed of nerves and of bones and
muscles; of bones and of nerves, not of flesh,
because it [the flesh] would be conquered by it; but
here we shall speak of the strength of the bone.

<summary>Leonardo outlines a systematic treatise on the nature of physical force, planning to define its origins, its "life span," and its anatomical manifestation through the interaction of nerves, bones, and muscles.</summary>

<keywords>mechanics, anatomy, force, compression, physiology, physics, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

->To refresh<-

For all the gold that you wish to refresh <note>In this context, "refreshing" refers to the process of refining, cleaning, or restoring the luster of gold, likely by removing impurities or surface oxidation.</note> quickly through boiling, you should take the water from that refreshing process in which old gold was refreshed; and from this,

to have the gold better refreshed, let it be refreshed in a basin <note>original: "catina." A large bowl or basin, specifically specified here to be without a tin lining.</note> that is not tinned, but in water that is ready to boil. And in this basin, refresh the gold inside with a "refresher"—I mean one made of wood, similar to the refreshers used to wash away the water from the gold. The dregs <term>morge|The oily or silty residue left over from refining or pressing processes, used here to help separate impurities from the metal.</term> refresh the gold, and it is quickly refreshed by them; and the water will be quickly refreshed by it. And with this, let nothing of the gold remain in the sediment; it shall be true <note>Leonardo frequently ends his technical notes with "fia vero" (it shall be true), asserting the effectiveness of the method described.</note>.

Many people labor over volumes that are cluttered with diverse arguments in order to reach a single conclusion regarding one aspect of a work; for which reason I shall not expand my discourse,

---

agreeing with those who avoid cluttering the minds of their listeners, through which means one arrives at the necessary conclusions.

---

Perspective is necessary to painting; it concerns how well a demonstration might be made, which should be ordered in such a way that it is clear and valid as a demonstration necessary to painting. <note>Leonardo views perspective not just as a tool, but as the mathematical foundation that makes painting a science.</note>

---

Furthermore, regarding the teeth of those who eat, which are cluttered by the nature of said teeth; in chewing, these things are varied. <note>This appears to be an analogy comparing the physical process of breaking down food with the mental process of digesting complex information.</note>

<margin>
I shall not lengthen these pages.
</margin>

->concerning the suspension of weights<-

If the weight is suspended at point **a**, and the midpoint is at **b**, the equilibrium will be at **c**. From this, one may derive such a demonstration <note>original: "dima," likely referring to a template or geometric proof</note> as follows:

---

Every weight will be so much heavier on the side of the perpendicular line <note>The vertical line representing the direction of gravity toward the center of the Earth.</note> as the weight is distant from the extremity of the balance; and it will be so much more as the weight is distant from its center, as was proven in the preceding chapter. And because the said perpendicular line is the true center of the Earth, it is concluded that the further it is distant from that center, the heavier the weight will be.

---

To divide the balance into a division of suspension <note>term: "sopesstamento" (suspension/counter-balancing) - the act of supporting a weight from below or hanging it to find its center of gravity.</note> from one to the other: one will be so much heavier than the other as the weight of one is further from the center of the balance than the other is.

<summary>Leonardo explains the principles of the lever and equilibrium, noting that a weight's perceived gravity increases as its horizontal distance from the central fulcrum increases.</summary>

<keywords>mechanics, balance, equilibrium, gravity, perpendicular line, physics, suspension</keywords>

# On Gravity

<note>Leonardo’s mechanical studies on the law of the lever, illustrating the principle of inverse proportionality between weights and their distances from the fulcrum. In these diagrams, Leonardo explores how different weights produce equivalent moments (torques) when positioned at specific distances.</note>

<note>Diagram 1: A horizontal lever with a central fulcrum 'a'. Three weights are illustrated at different positions: 2 units at distance 6, 4 units at distance 3, and 6 units at distance 2. Each combination results in a balanced state (2 × 6 = 12, 4 × 3 = 12, 6 × 2 = 12).</note>

<note>Diagram 2: A similar study using weights of 1, 4, and 8 units. Weight 1 is at distance 8, weight 4 at distance 2, and weight 8 at distance 1 (1 × 8 = 8, 4 × 2 = 8, 8 × 1 = 8).</note>

<note>Diagram 3: A compound lever system where one bar supports another, demonstrating the distribution of weights (1, 2, 4, and 8) through a multi-stage mechanism.</note>

If one of the parts of the <term>balance</term><note>original: "bilancia"</note>
which <insert>is composed</insert> of different weights, even though
they are of the same <insert>substance</insert>, should be of
<note>The letters n m likely refer to points on a geometric diagram or specific units of measure.</note> n m . n m . n m . n m . n m . n m
<term>proportions</term><note>original: "p r r", likely an abbreviation for "proporzioni"</note> of the arms of the weights of the

by the <term>inverse</term><note>original: "contrario"</note> arm, the one to the

<note>Leonardo is describing the fundamental law of statics: for a lever to be in equilibrium, the weights must be inversely proportional to their distances from the pivot (fulcrum). If you double the weight, you must halve the distance to maintain balance.</note>

### Drawing Description
The diagram at the top of the page illustrates a mechanical problem involving a horizontal lever or balance beam. 

- **The Beam and Weights:** A horizontal beam is depicted with five weights suspended from it. The weights are represented as squares and labeled with the numbers **4**, **1**, **5**, **3**, and a final symbol resembling a cross (**+**).
- **Proportional Markers:** Above the beam are several vertical lines of varying heights, marked with numbers and letters (including **5**, **5**, **m**, **e**). These likely represent the distances from a fulcrum or reference points for calculating moments.
- **Mathematical Annotations:** Smaller numbers (**1**, **1**, **m**, **1**, **2**) are placed along the vertical axes. Thin curved lines beneath the beam indicate the potential arc of movement or deflection.
- **Calculations:** In the right margin, Leonardo has written a sequence of numbers (**1-1-4-11**) and a fractional calculation (**21/5**) that relates to the sum of weights or distances shown in the diagram.

# On the Equilibrium of Weights

<note>Leonardo is exploring the fundamental Law of the Lever: that a weight's force increases as it moves further from the center of the balance (the fulcrum).</note>

If the weight *m. f. c.* were equal to the weight *d. a.*,
it seems to me that, the spaces being equal, they
would say that the heaviness of *m. f. c.* is still equal
in comparison to *d. a.*, as appears through the
example mentioned above.

---

### The Principle of Distance and Gravity

An object will be so much lighter as it
is further from the center of the balance,
and on the contrary, it will be so much heavier as
it is closer to the center of the balance,
as appears through the example above; because
the weight *m. f. c.* would be heaviness *m.* and the <term>shadow</term><note>original: "onbra". Leonardo uses the term "shadow" (ombra) to refer to the "potential arm" or the horizontal distance from the fulcrum to the vertical line of a weight's descent.</note> *n.*
would be the shadow *d.*, it would be heaviness *f.* and the shadow *n.* would be
gravity *m. f. c.*, it would be gravity *m.* and of shadow
*n.* would be gravity *d. m.* And because the part *m.* would be
more distant from the center through which *n.* would be
the shadow through *n.*, and *n.* would be the gravity *d.*, and *m.* would be
the gravity *m.*, and the *d.* would be the gravity *n.* and *m.*

<note>Leonardo's phrasing here is repetitive and dense, reflecting his process of working through the geometric relationships between the physical weight and its "potential" weight based on its position (the "shadow"). He is essentially trying to define the moment of a force.</note>

# [Proportional Studies of the Lever and Triangle]

<note>Top diagram: A study of a graduated lever or balance beam with suspended weights at either end. The left weight is marked with three "3"s, and the right weight is marked "12". A series of intersecting arcs and vertical lines, labeled with numbers like "4+" and "12" and "10", suggest a study of proportional weight distribution or centers of gravity.</note>

That line **d n** <note>Referring to a segment in the geometric construction</note> brings the area <note>original: "aria", a common variant for "area" in Leonardo's notes</note> into the same proportion <margin>○</margin>
as is created between **m t** or between **m n**.
With the cube <note>original: "rubo", likely a technical shorthand or corruption of "cubo" (cube) or "rubeo" (red/marked line)</note> of **f d n**, it is just as equal. <note>original: "equo", meaning equality or equilibrium</note>

<note>Bottom diagram: A geometric construction of a right-angled triangle with a vertical altitude. The vertices are labeled "d", "n", and "m". A point "t" is marked on the base line "dm" near the right angle "m". A small circle is drawn at the top vertex "n".</note>

<note>Leonardo is applying the principles of Euclidean geometry to physical mechanics. By comparing the segments of the triangle (d, n, m), he is attempting to find a mathematical rule that governs how a weight's influence changes as it moves along a lever arm.</note>

<term>Proportion (proporzione)</term>: The comparative relation of one part to another, essential to Leonardo's "Law of the Lever" where weight and distance from the fulcrum are inversely related.

<term>Area (aria)</term>: In this context, it likely refers to the spatial distribution or the geometric "sweep" of the lever as it moves through an arc.

# Page 143 / 85

<note>Top diagram: A study of accelerated motion. A horizontal bar is marked with units of '1'. Curved lines descend from these markers to a vertical axis on the right, which is annotated with the sequence of square numbers: 1, 4, 9, 16, 25. This demonstrates Leonardo's early grasp of the law of squares—that the distance traveled by a falling body increases by the square of the time elapsed.</note>

<note>Middle diagram: A lever or balance system. Two weights are shown in boxes labeled '9' and '36'. These numbers correspond to the squares of their distances (3 and 6 units) from the central pivot, exploring the mathematical proportions of equilibrium and momentum.</note>

<note>Bottom diagram: Two concentric arcs labeled 'o', 'd', 'o' and 'm', 'n', 'm', used to analyze the properties of curvilinear paths compared to straight lines.</note>

Motion made along a **straight line** <note>original: "righa retta"</note> is of a different **nature** than that which is made along a **circular curved line**.

---

Every **weight** <note>original: "peso"</note> that moves through the **air** is divided into 4 parts, considered by all the **powers** <note>original: "potenze" - referring to the physical forces or factors involved</note> that move it: that is, the **effort** <note>original: "sforzo"</note> and the **force** of the **mover** <note>original: "motora" - the source of the motion</note>, and the weight of the **thing moved**.

<margin>
<note>The margin contains a list of technical terms used in the study: motion, straight line, nature, circular curved line, weight, air, powers, effort, force, mover, thing moved.</note>
</margin>

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the fundamental distinction between linear and circular motion and outlines the four primary factors—effort, force, the mover, and the weight—that govern the movement of an object through the air. ### [Mechanical Principles]

<note>Leonardo wrote the following text in his famous "mirror script" (from right to left). It discusses the fundamental law of the lever: the trade-off between the distance an object moves and the length of the mechanism moving it.</note>

that which will travel a short distance will be the long <term>lever arm</term> by the half,
and short will be that which, through the straight path, they make move
by the said long distance, therefore...
<note>original: "che s’anderà corta fia el lungo per la meza / e corta fia quella per lo dretto fanno muovere / per detta lunga dunque." Leonardo is describing the inverse relationship between force and distance; a "long" movement at one end of a machine results in a "short" but more powerful movement at the other.</note>

**Technical Terms:**
*   <term>Short (corta)</term>: referring to the distance of travel or the displacement of a weight.
*   <term>Long (lungo)</term>: referring to the length of a lever or the amount of rope pulled.
*   <term>Half (meza)</term>: a proportional unit of measurement.
*   <term>Straight (dretto)</term>: refers to linear motion or a direct path.
*   <term>Move (mouere)</term>: the act of mechanical displacement.
*   <term>Proportion (proporzione)</term>: the mathematical ratio that governs mechanical advantage.

<summary>This page presents a series of scales used to calculate mathematical ratios, accompanied by a brief note on the relationship between distance and leverage. It captures Leonardo’s attempt to formalize the "law of the lever" through geometric proportions.</summary>

<keywords>proportions, mechanics, leverage, mathematical ratios, mirror writing, distance and force, Codex Forster</keywords>

<margin>
If the weight is
1 and there are 4
cords, each
cord will
be 1/4.

If the weight is
2, each
cord will be 1/2.

And if it is 1/2,
it will be 1/8.

</margin>

The <term>force</term><note>original: "uirtù"; in this context, Leonardo uses 'virtue' to mean the physical power, tension, or functional capacity of the cord</note> of cord .f. is as much as that which sustains it. If .f. is .1/2. and .e. is with .f., it will be .1. because it will be .1/2. to the .1/2. And likewise, it will be .1/4. to the .1/4. because the force of the cord is divisible by its proportional <term>weights</term><note>original: "grauezze"; literally 'heavinesses' or loads</note> .d. Therefore, if .f. and .a. are .1/2. and .e. [and] .d. are .1/2., the weight .1. will be placed upon 4 times .1/4., and 4 times .1/4. is .1. 

And if the weight were .2., then it would be .1/2. for each of the 4. And if the weight is .3., then it would be .3/4. for each of the 4. And if the weight were .1/2., then it would be .1/8. for each of the 4. And if it were .1/4., then it would be .1/16. for each of the 4. And if it were .1/8., then it would be .1/32. for each of the 4. And if it were .1/16., then it would be .1/64. for each of the 4. And proceed thus to infinity. The weight will always be divided by 4, and from the force of one of the cords, the whole <unclear>divided</unclear> weight will be made manifest.

<note>Leonardo is attempting to quantify the resistance and weight of air by comparing it to a solid material like lead. ### Studies on Proportional Scales

<note>This page features a series of horizontal scales or "rulers" demonstrating fractional divisions and proportions, essential for Leonardo's engineering and artistic measurements.</note>

1.  **First scale:** marked with **1/6** and the number **8** on the left; **2/6** and a symbol resembling **v** on the right.

<note>Leonardo’s mirror-written note "q — di proportione s’intende" serves as a summary for the visual exercises above. <summary>Leonardo presents a series of diagrammatic scales to illustrate the mathematical division of lines into fractions such as halves, thirds, and sixths. These exercises reflect his foundational interest in proportion as the governing principle of both nature and mechanics.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page's focus on mathematical proportions, Leonardo now applies these principles to the mechanics of a balance beam and the distribution of weight.</meta>

Whoever divides the weight between **m** and **n** into equal parts, it will weigh half the weight.

---

# mechanics

<note>Diagram of a lever or balance beam. A horizontal beam is suspended at a fulcrum 'c'. To the left at point 'n' hangs a weight labeled '5'. To the right are points 'd', 'a', and 'f'. 'a' is the midpoint between 'd' and 'f'. Points 'd' and 'f' have small marks under them, representing weights. The diagram illustrates a study on the center of gravity and lever equilibrium.</note>

Let these weights which are at **d** and **f** be joined together.
Those at **d** and **f** make 5, and they are in the middle of **d f**. The weight 5 which is at **n** is of the same quantity as the weight which is at **d f**. And for this reason, the unified weight will be of opposite parity <note>original: "opposta parità"; a term used by Leonardo to describe a state of equilibrium or equal counterbalancing</note> to weight **n**, and the center of such weight shall be at **a**; and at this point **a**, the weight shall be equal to weight **n**, because the arm **n c** is equal to the arm **c a**.

---

<margin>
<note>Stylized capital letter 'P' or a diagrammatic symbol marking a new proposition.</note>
</margin>

Half of the arm of **n** shall be as **d f** doubled at **a**, and half of the arm of **n** doubled [at] **d** and **a** will be.
Those two of these weights shall be equal to the weight at **n**; and if doubled, it shall be of opposite parity; that arm shall be equal to the weight **n**.
And **n** is in its arm of opposite parity if it be equal to **d c**. But if the weight **n** be doubled, it will perpetually be of such doubled weight; the weight shall be at **a** and shall be equal to the doubled weight at **n**, because the doubled **d c** is equal to **n c**.

<term>mechanics</term> <note>original: "meccanica"; the branch of applied mathematics dealing with motion and forces producing motion.</note>
<term>weights</term> <note>original: "pesi"; the heavy objects used in these experiments to test the laws of balance.</note>
<term>arm</term> <note>original: "braccio"; the distance from the fulcrum of a lever to the point where the force or weight is applied.</note>
<term>center of such weight</term> <note>original: "centro di tal peso"; effectively the center of gravity, where the total weight of a system is concentrated.</note>
<term>opposite parity</term> <note>original: "opposta parità"; a state where weights and distances on either side of a fulcrum are balanced.</note>
<term>doubled</term> <note>original: "raddoppiato"; referring to the multiplication of weight or distance to maintain equilibrium.</note>

<summary>This page explores the principles of the lever, demonstrating how two separate weights (d and f) can be mathematically and physically treated as a single weight acting from their midpoint (a) to achieve balance against a counterweight (n).</summary>

<note>Leonardo is calculating the inverse relationship between weight and distance from the fulcrum (the center). In this mechanical principle, a smaller weight at a greater distance can balance a larger weight closer to the center.</note>

<summary>This page explores the physical properties of beams and the mathematical laws of the lever, demonstrating how weight and distance from a central pivot point determine equilibrium.</summary>

--- Page 318 ---
<meta>This page shifts from the geometric proportions of falling water streams discussed on the previous page to the turbulent behavior of water as it strikes an embankment or dam, focusing on the repetitive, circular motions of erosion.</meta>

### On the Breaking of Water

The water that strikes upward at the base of the embankment <note>original: "argine"; a levee or dam wall.</note> to return to the sea along with the primary water of that <note>Symbol: circle with horizontal line</note> impact, bounces upward and strikes back <note>original: "rinveste"; a technical term used by the author to describe the water doubling back or striking again.</note> with the new water that falls upon it from above.
<margin>3/1+</margin> And there it strikes back from top to bottom, and strikes back into its own center, and strikes back against the wall of that embankment; and by its return, it strikes back against the water coming from above, and from top to bottom it strikes back at the bed <note>original: "fondo"; the floor or bottom of the water body.</note> which was displaced by the first impact. And as this ball <note>Dark circular mark; likely referring to a vortex or "palla d'acqua"</note> of water strikes back, it hollows out the bed and strikes back into the embankment to return to the midst of its own water; and there, by a similar return, it strikes back against the water that comes near it, and there, striking back, it strikes back up the embankment and strikes back near its own water, and striking back, it strikes back at the embankment to return to the midst of its own water, and striking back, it strikes back...

<summary>This page describes the complex, recursive turbulence of water hitting an embankment, detailing how the "striking back" of the current creates vortices that erode the bed and the wall.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, erosion, turbulence, embankment, vortices, fluid dynamics, water impact</keywords>
